To fix Power Analysis Check

What we found

We detected 1 potential power analysis.

Show details

We used the LLM model ‘vllm/DeepSeek-V41-Flash-1017-FP8’ to check the contents of 1 paragraph that contained words suggesting they might contain power analyses.

Some essential information could not be detected: effect_size, software

power_type statistical_test sample_size alpha_level power effect_size effect_size_metric software
apriori other 130 0.05 0.89 NA other NA
text
An a priori simulation-based power analysis for the cross-classified multilevel models, parameterised using variance components and fixed-effect estimates from Muth et al. (2015), indicated that a design with 130 participants each rating 30 artworks yields ≥ 0.89 power to detect the target fixed effects for interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect at α = 0.05, and ≥ 0.80 power for smaller effects under the same model structure.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 1 imprecise p value out of 13 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p > .05 Quadratic and cubic terms did not improve any of the three models (all p > .05), so linear models were retained (see Supplement S2).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 4 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Text Sentence
p > .05 Quadratic and cubic terms did not improve any of the three models (all p > .05), so linear models were retained (see Supplement S2).
p = .22 In joint models including both predictors, insight confidence remained a positive predictor of interest (b = 0.29, SE = 0.09, β = .18) and emotional intensity (b = 0.31, SE = 0.08, β = .18), but not cognitive affect (b = 0.09, SE = 0.07, p = .22).
p = .16 Semantic distance was no longer significant for interest (b = -0.13, p = .16) or emotional intensity (b = -0.14, p = .11), and its effect on cognitive affect (b = -0.16, SE = 0.07, p = .03) was significant but did not meet the corrected threshold (see Supplement S6).
p = .11 Semantic distance was no longer significant for interest (b = -0.13, p = .16) or emotional intensity (b = -0.14, p = .11), and its effect on cognitive affect (b = -0.16, SE = 0.07, p = .03) was significant but did not meet the corrected threshold (see Supplement S6).

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. setwd() calls were found in 2 code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In all code files, libraries/imports were loaded in one block.

Packages / Dependencies

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 10 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

Metacheck can’t automatically establish what caused the error, or how it should be improved.

  • 4.MSTAT_Int.R

The error message of each script is in the table above.

Other problems in the files

  • 1.Clean_raw_data.R calls setwd(), which ties the script to a folder on the author’s computer. We replaced the call with one that does nothing so the script could run; it should be removed from the script.
  • Impute_MSTAT.R calls setwd(), which ties the script to a folder on the author’s computer. We replaced the call with one that does nothing so the script could run; it should be removed from the script.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

This module assesses whether the paper’s code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 8 R code files.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first), source() calls, and numeric filename prefixes.

7 4.MSTAT_Int.R Impute_MSTAT.R dependency
8 VAIAK_imputation.R — none

File paths

2 data paths in the code do not work in the repository as deposited. We pointed them to where the file is, so the code could run: Datasets/QIP_results/QIP_results.csv to ../Datasets/QIP_results.csv (in 5.Image_feature_moderation.R); Datasets/vaiak_imputed_scores.csv to ../Datasets/vaiak_imputed_scores.csv (in 6.VAIAK_int.R). The code should be changed, because as written it does not find these files.

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 8 scripts: 4 ran without error, 4 errored, 0 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions. 1 script uses an object or function that only another script defines, so in the second run that script was loaded first, in the same session: 2.RQ1.R after 1.Clean_raw_data.R. Such a script should source() the other itself, since it does not run on its own.

a script hit an object '...' not found error, indicating it expected a variable another script defines. We inferred which script supplies it and re-ordered so that script runs first, and re-ran once. The outcomes above are from that corrected run.

setwd() in the code

2 scripts call setwd(). This is bad practice: it hardcodes where the code must run (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine) and breaks the moment the code is run anywhere else. To run the code we replaced these calls with a statement that does nothing. Any such script that then ran is reproducible only after ignoring a setwd() that should not have been in the code and needs to be fixed.

Reproducible after removing setwd(): 1.Clean_raw_data.R, Impute_MSTAT.R.

File setwd() removed Path(s) it set
1.Clean_raw_data.R 1 (non-literal)
Impute_MSTAT.R 1 (non-literal)

> total_var       <- sum(null_vc$vcov)
> icc_participant <- null_vc$vcov[null_vc$grp == "participant_id"] / total_var
> icc_artwork     <- null_vc$vcov[null_vc$grp == "artwork_number"] / total_var
> icc_residual    <- null_vc$vcov[null_vc$grp == "Residual"]       / total_var
> model_main <- lmer(
>   response_numeric ~ mean_distance_std +
>     (1 | participant_id) + (1 | artwork_number),
>   data = analysis_data, REML = FALSE
> )
> model_rs_participant <- lmer(
>   response_numeric ~ mean_distance_std +
>     (mean_distance_std | participant_id) + (1 | artwork_number),
>   data = analysis_data, REML = FALSE
> )
> model_rs_artwork <- lmer(
>   response_numeric ~ mean_distance_std +
>     (1 | participant_id) + (mean_distance_std | artwork_number),
>   data = analysis_data, REML = FALSE
> )
> rs_participant_singular <- isSingular(model_rs_participant)
> rs_artwork_singular     <- isSingular(model_rs_artwork)
> comp_null_main    <- anova(null_model,  model_main,           test = "Chisq")
> comp_main_rs_part <- anova(model_main,  model_rs_participant, test = "Chisq")
> comp_main_rs_art  <- anova(model_main,  model_rs_artwork,     test = "Chisq")
> safe_p <- function(comp) {
>   p <- tryCatch(comp$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2], error = function(e) NA_real_)
>   ifelse(is.na(p), 1, p)
> }
> p_part <- if (!rs_participant_singular) safe_p(comp_main_rs_part) else 1
> p_art  <- if (!rs_artwork_singular)     safe_p(comp_main_rs_art)  else 1
> if (p_part < 0.05 && (p_art >= 0.05 || !rs_artwork_singular == FALSE)) {
>   best_model      <- model_rs_participant
>   best_model_name <- "Random Slopes by Participant"
> } else if (p_art < 0.05 && p_part >= 0.05) {
>   best_model      <- model_rs_artwork
>   best_model_name <- "Random Slopes by Artwork"
> } else if (p_part < 0.05 && p_art < 0.05) {
>   best_model      <- if (AIC(model_rs_participant) < AIC(model_rs_artwork)) model_rs_participant else model_rs_artwork
>   best_model_name <- if (AIC(model_rs_participant) < AIC(model_rs_artwork)) "Random Slopes by Participant" else "Random Slopes by Artwork"
> } else {
>   best_model      <- model_main
>   best_model_name <- "Intercept only"
> }
> cat("Best model:", best_model_name, "\n")
Best model: Random Slopes by Participant 
> model_ml_std <- switch(best_model_name,
>                        "Random Slopes by Participant" = lmer(
>                          response_std ~ mean_distance_std +
>                            (mean_distance_std | participant_id) + (1 | artwork_number),
>                          data = analysis_data, REML = FALSE),
>                        "Random Slopes by Artwork" = lmer(

>                        lmer(
>                          response_std ~ mean_distance_std +

> artwork_level <- analysis_data %>%

>     sd_confidence   = sd(response_numeric),

> lm_trial     <- lm(response_numeric ~ mean_distance,     data = analysis_data)

> get_r2_safe <- function(m) {
>   as.numeric(tryCatch(performance::r2(m)$R2_marginal, error = function(e) NA_real_))
> }

> get_ols_row <- function(label, model, term, data) {
>   tidy_m   <- broom::tidy(model)
>   glance_m <- broom::glance(model)
>   tidy_m %>% filter(term == !!term) %>%
>     transmute(Analysis = label, b = round(estimate, 2), SE = round(std.error, 2),
>               t = round(statistic, 2), df = round(glance_m$df.residual, 0),
>               p = signif(p.value, 3), R2 = round(glance_m$r.squared, 2))
> }

> model_quadratic <- lmer(
>   response_numeric ~ poly(mean_distance_std, 2) +
>     (1 | participant_id) + (1 | artwork_number),
>   data = analysis_data, REML = FALSE

> poly_comp  <- anova(best_model, model_quadratic, test = "Chisq")
> aic_linear <- AIC(best_model)
> aic_quad   <- AIC(model_quadratic)
> re_participant         <- ranef(best_model)$participant_id[, 1, drop = TRUE]
> re_artwork             <- ranef(best_model)$artwork_number[, 1, drop = TRUE]
> shapiro_re_participant <- shapiro.test(re_participant)
> shapiro_re_artwork     <- shapiro.test(re_artwork)
> participant_extreme      <- which(abs(re_participant) > 2 * sd(re_participant))
> artwork_extreme          <- which(abs(re_artwork)     > 2 * sd(re_artwork))
> perc_participant_extreme <- 100 * length(participant_extreme) / length(re_participant)
> perc_artwork_extreme     <- 100 * length(artwork_extreme)     / length(re_artwork)
> overall_lm    <- lm(response_numeric ~ mean_distance_std, data = analysis_data)
> overall_slope <- coef(overall_lm)[2]
> safe_slope <- function(df) {
>   df_clean <- df %>% filter(!is.na(response_numeric), !is.na(mean_distance_std))
>   if (nrow(df_clean) < 2 || n_distinct(df_clean$mean_distance_std) < 2) return(tibble(slope = NA_real_))
>   tibble(slope = coef(lm(response_numeric ~ mean_distance_std, data = df_clean))[2])
> }
> within_participant       <- analysis_data %>% group_by(participant_id) %>% do(safe_slope(.)) %>% ungroup()
> within_participant_valid <- within_participant %>% filter(!is.na(slope))
> prop_part_same_dir       <- mean(sign(within_participant_valid$slope) == sign(overall_slope), na.rm = TRUE)

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

Each participant gave one description per artwork, so semantic distance cannot capture the breadth of meanings held by an individual viewer.

Data were collected online via the Prolific platform from 130 participants (M_age = 36.9 years, SD = 12.8, range = 20-77; 83 male, 46 female, 1 undisclosed).

Seven participants were excluded due to incomplete data, leaving a final sample of 123 participants included in all analyses.

Eligibility on Prolific was restricted to healthy adults from a set of Western countries, as the stimulus set comprised predominantly Western artworks and cultural familiarity with these artistic traditions was expected to influence interpretive and aesthetic responses (see Supplement S14 for the list of countries).

Participants were asked to describe any insights they gained from an artwork and if they felt unable to do so, they were instructed to provide a purely physical description instead.

Based on feedback from a pilot study, participants were randomly assigned to one of two counterbalancing groups to reduce workload.

Participants were instructed to keep descriptions brief, but no explicit word limit was imposed.

The experimental session began with a pre-task questionnaire in which participants completed the VAIAK Part A to assess art interest.

After all the 60 trials, participants completed a post-task questionnaire comprising the MSTAT-II to assess ambiguity tolerance, which was administered at the end of the study to avoid priming or biasing participants with respect to ambiguity.

The sample was recruited via Prolific and restricted to participants from Western cultural backgrounds, and the stimulus set comprised 60 predominantly Western artworks, which limits generalisation to other populations and artistic traditions.

Informed consent was obtained from all participants of the study via Prolific before beginning the task.

Ethics approval statement:

The study was approved by the Leiden University Research Ethics Committee (V1-5951), complied with the ethical standards as laid down in the 1964 Declaration of Helsinki.

id title template
njdx5 Who Appreciates Ambiguity in Visual Art? OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

title Who Appreciates Ambiguity in Visual Art?
id njdx5
link https://osf.io/njdx5
date_created 2025-10-23T12:44:15.046992
date_modified 2025-11-04T11:21:14.850031
date_registered 2025-10-23T12:44:15.024162
embargo_end_date 2026-10-23T00:00:00Z
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-njdx5-v1
research_questions Research Question 1: What is the relationship between semantic distance and insight-confidence?
We predict a significant negative relationship between semantic distance and insight-confidence, with a higher semantic distance associated with lower insight-confidence (directional hypothesis).
Research Question 2: How does SeIns influence aesthetic appreciation?
We hypothesise that artwork-level semantic distance will be positively correlated with the three aesthetic appreciation measures (cognitive affect, emotional intensity and interest), whilst insight-confidence will be negatively correlated with these same measures, such that higher semantic distance and lower insight-confidence both correspond to higher appreciation scores (directional hypotheses; Muth et al., 2015).
We predict that individual differences in ambiguity tolerance, as measured by MSTAT-II (McLain, 2009), will moderate the semantic distance-appreciation and insight-confidence-appreciation associations across all three aesthetic measures. Specifically, participants higher in ambiguity tolerance are expected to show stronger positive associations between artwork-level semantic distance and their ratings of cognitive affect, emotional intensity, and interest, and stronger negative associations between artwork-level insight-confidence and these aesthetic ratings (directional hypotheses; Muth et al., 2015).
study_type Observational Study - Data is collected from study subjects that are not randomly assigned to a treatment. This includes surveys, “natural experiments,” and regression discontinuity designs.
blinding No blinding is involved in this study.
study_design_overview This experiment uses a mixed factorial design to examine aesthetic responses and cognitive processing of visual art. 150 artworks were initially selected from various online galleries such as the Rijksmuseum or the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) and from digital scans of artworks from art catalogues. From 150 artworks, 60 were selected using PCA-based diversity sampling. Forty-three quantitative image properties were calculated using the Aesthetics Toolbox (Redies et al., 2025) and standardized. PCA retained components explaining 80% variance (k = 10), weighted by explained variance ratios. Diversity scores were calculated as mean Mahalanobis distances in the weighted PC space. Artworks were stratified into five quintiles by diversity score, with 12 randomly sampled from each bin, ensuring balanced representation across the visual feature space.
Participants view the 60 artworks for a fixed duration of 6 seconds each, with two types of response tasks: (1) rating ‘interest’, ‘cognitive affect’ and ‘emotional intensity’ on Likert scales (1-7), or (2) providing artwork descriptions and rating insight-confidence of that description (1-7).
Each participant completes one task type per artwork, with 30 artworks assigned to each condition. The artwork-task pairings are systematically counterbalanced between participants (Group A: artworks 1-30 get interest/emotion questions, artworks 31-60 get cognitive-affect/description+insight-confidence questions; Group B receives the reverse assignment). After this counterbalanced assignment, all 60 trials are randomly shuffled for each participant to control for order effects.
Participants also complete pre- and post-session questionnaires: the VAIAK Part-A (Visual Art Interest and Knowledge; Specker et al., 2023) questionnaire measuring individual differences in art experience and expertise before the main task, and the MSTAT-II (McLain, 2009) measuring ambiguity tolerance after the main task. This design allows examination of how different cognitive demands (evaluative ratings vs. interpretive description) influence aesthetic appreciation whilst controlling for artwork-specific effects through counterbalancing and order effects through randomisation.
existing_data Registration prior to accessing the data
data_collection_procedures The study will be conducted online on the Prolific platform from a general population. They will be paid 9£ for the study and it approximately takes 50minutes to an hour.
We have decided to select healthy participants from a western population as most of the artworks we show are western artworks and individual differences is a variable of interest.
sample_size Our target sample is 130 participants
sample_size_rationale This study employs a multilevel design with participants at Level 2 and individual artwork ratings at Level 1. Each participant provides interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect ratings for 30 artworks from a pool of 60 artworks. With 130 planned participants, this yields 3,900 total observations per primary outcome analysis. This number was selected due to time and budgetary constraints.
Power analysis for the primary multilevel analyses was conducted using simulation-based approaches appropriate for cross-classified designs with random effects of both participants and artworks, implemented in R using custom Monte Carlo simulations informed by the ‘simr’ package (Green and MacLeod, 2016). The analysis utilised variance components and effect sizes extracted from a previous study (Muth et al., 2015), with derived effect sizes of β = 0.26 for ‘interest’ ratings, β = 0.33 for ‘emotional intensity’ ratings, and β = 0.23 for ‘cognitive affect’ ratings. Models included only random intercepts for participants and artworks, as variance component extraction from the Muth et al. (2015) data revealed negligible random slope variance (< 0.05 for all outcomes), indicating minimal between-participant and between-artwork heterogeneity in the effect of ‘insight-confidence’. Monte Carlo simulations (1,000 iterations per sample size condition, α = 0.05) indicated that 130 participants provided 91% power (95% CI [0.89, 0.93]) for interest ratings, 89% power (95% CI [0.88, 0.91]) for emotional intensity ratings, and 93% power (95% CI [0.92, 0.95]) for cognitive affect ratings.
Sensitivity analyses were conducted to determine the minimum detectable effect sizes at 80% power with N = 130. Results indicate that the planned sample can reliably detect effects as small as β = 0.22 for ‘interest’, β = 0.28 for ‘emotional intensity’, and β = 0.19 for ‘cognitive affect’, all achieving 80% power. This demonstrates that our design is well-powered not only for the pilot effect sizes but also for smaller effects, providing insight-confidence in detecting meaningful relationships between insight-confidence and aesthetic appreciation responses whilst accounting for the nested data structure.
manipulated_variables It is an observational study and nothing is being manipulated
measured_variables Primary Outcome Variables
Aesthetic appreciation will be measured using three measures from Muth et al. (2015) assessing emotional intensity, cognitive affect, and interest on 7-point Likert scales for each of the 60 artworks viewed.
Insight-confidence will be measured on a 7-point Likert scale for artworks in the description condition, operationalised as participants’ confidence in their interpretations.
Semantic Distance will be computed as semantic distance scores between participants’ artwork descriptions using sentence BERT embeddings (BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5 model). Cosine similarity will be calculated between all pairwise combinations of description embeddings for each artwork, with semantic distance defined as 1 minus cosine similarity, providing a computational measure of group-level interpretive differences.

Secondary Outcome Variables
Ambiguity-Tolerance will be measured using the MSTAT-II scale (McLain, 2009).
Art interest and knowledge will be measured using the VAIAK-A (Specker et al., 2023).
indices VAIAK Part-A Score will be calculated according to the standard VAIAK scoring procedure.

MSTAT-II Ambiguity Tolerance Score will be calculated as the sum of all items according to McLain (2009) scoring guidelines.

Group-Level Semantic Distance will be operationalised as the mean pairwise semantic distance score between all participants’ descriptions for each artwork, computed using cosine similarity of sentence embeddings (sentence BERT transformer model BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5), where semantic distance = 1 - cosine similarity.
statistical_tests Research Question 1:What is the relationship between semantic distance and individual insight-confidence?
This hypothesis will be tested using a cross-classified multilevel model where individual insight-confidence ratings (Level 1) are nested within both participants and artworks (Level 2). Artwork-level mean semantic distance (calculated as the mean pairwise cosine distance between all descriptions of each artwork) serves as the primary Level 2 artwork predictor. The model includes random intercepts for participants and artworks to account for individual baseline insight-confidence and artwork-specific effects. This tests whether artworks that elicit more interpretively divergent descriptions (higher semantic distance) are associated with lower individual insight-confidence ratings.

Research Question 2: How does SeIns influence aesthetic appreciation?
We hypothesise that semantic distance will be positively correlated with the aesthetic appreciation measures (cognitive affect, emotional intensity and interest), while insight-confidence will be negatively correlated with these same measures, such that higher semantic distance and lower insight-confidence both independently correspond to higher appreciation scores (directional hypotheses; Muth et al., 2015).
These hypotheses will be tested using cross-classified multilevel regression analyses. Due to the counterbalanced design, interest and emotional intensity ratings are provided by different participants than those who generate semantic distance and insight-confidence measures for each artwork. Therefore, for interest and emotional intensity, we examine artwork-level relationships only: individual ratings (Level 1) are modeled as a function of artwork-level mean semantic distance and mean insight-confidence (Level 2 artwork predictors, aggregated from the description condition participants), with participant-level ambiguity tolerance (MSTAT-II) as a Level 2 participant predictor and cross-level interactions.
For cognitive affect, participants provide both ratings and descriptions for the same artworks, enabling examination of both individual-level and artwork-level relationships. The model includes individual insight-confidence as a Level 1 predictor alongside the artwork-level predictors described above.
All models include random intercepts for both participants and artworks, as well as random slopes for artwork-level semantic distance and artwork-level insight-confidence by participants, allowing individual sensitivity to these features to vary. For cognitive affect, random slopes for individual insight-confidence by artworks are also included to model artwork-specific variation in the insight-confidence-appreciation relationship. If full random-slope models fail to converge, we will simplify the random structure following Barr et al. (2013), removing slopes with negligible variance components sequentially while retaining all fixed effects.

We predict that individual differences in ambiguity tolerance, as measured by MSTAT-II (McLain, 2009), will moderate both the semantic distance-appreciation and insight-confidence-appreciation associations across all three aesthetic measures. This will be tested by incorporating individual MSTAT-II scores and their interactions with artwork-level mean semantic distance and mean insight-confidence ratings into the multilevel framework, with participants higher in ambiguity tolerance showing stronger positive associations between artwork semantic distance and their aesthetic ratings (cognitive affect, emotional intensity, and interest), and stronger negative associations between artwork insight-confidence and these aesthetic ratings (directional hypotheses; Muth et al., 2015).

Lakens, Daniël (2024). “When and How to Deviate From a Preregistration.” Collabra: Psychology, 10(1), 117094. doi:10.1525/collabra.117094.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.
