How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Previous work in psychology of aesthetics suggests that people tend to appreciate artworks they subjectively perceive as ambiguous. Yet ambiguity of an artwork is typically measured through viewers' own ratings or qualitative measures which are well suited to within-person questions, but they do not, on their own, describe how much viewers disagree about what a work means. Defining ambiguity as the degree to which an artwork affords multiple interpretations, we introduce a computational, description-based measure that quantifies this multiplicity as the semantic distance between viewers' descriptions. We complemented this with a metacognitive measure, insight confidence, which captures how confident viewers are in their own interpretations. We examined how these measures relate to aesthetic appreciation, operationalised as interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect. In an online study (N = 123), participants viewed artworks for 6 seconds and for each individual artwork either described them, rated their insight confidence and provided cognitive affect ratings, or they provided interest and emotional intensity ratings. Across artworks, those eliciting more divergent descriptions (greater semantic distance) received lower mean insight confidence. Contrary to preregistered predictions, such artworks also received lower aesthetic appreciation. These results indicate aesthetic appreciation is more strongly related to how certain a viewer is about what they are viewing rather than the degree of ambiguity of an artwork. Methodologically, the approach offers a way to quantify the divergence of openended verbal interpretations across viewers, alongside a measure of viewers' confidence in their own interpretation.

Introduction

Our visual environment contains dynamic scenes with multiple objects and overlapping visual features, making them challenging for us to perceive (Malcolm et al., 2016). Despite this complexity, humans usually recognise objects and scenes without difficulty (Peelen et al., 2024). When viewing artworks, however, viewers often encounter stimuli that deliberately resist singular interpretation. The central question of this preregistered online study is whether, and how, the ease of arriving at a stable and coherent interpretation of an artwork influences the aesthetic experience it elicits. Kazimir Malevich's Black Square (1915) for example, invites interpretations built on viewers' own associations with colour, texture and shape (Figure 1A), whereas Caravaggio's Basket of Fruit (1599) mostly constrains interpretation to what is depicted (Figure 1B).

Processing fluency accounts propose that appreciation increases with ease of processing (Reber et al., 2004), but fluency alone cannot explain responses to artworks that invite interpretive effort (Mayer & Landwehr, 2018). Predictive coding theories of art suggest that visual features do not directly produce aesthetic responses but are constrained by higher-level predictions about composition, style and meaning (Redies et al., 2025; Richter et al., 2024; Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). In this framework, observers generate predictions about visual input at multiple levels, from low-level feature-based expectations, through mid-level configurational predictions, up to high-level abstract or concrete semantic predictions (Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). In this view, what distinguishes Malevich's and Caravaggio's works is not only their visual structure but the breadth of high-level predictions they afford: Malevich supports many competing semantic interpretations, whereas Caravaggio narrows interpretation to a small set of object-centred readings.

Figure 1

Examples of an Ambiguous and a Representational Artwork Note. (A) Black Square (Malevich, 1915). (B) Basket of Fruit (Caravaggio, 1599).

Reproduced from a digital image on Wikimedia Commons (PD-Art).

We here refer to this breadth of viable high-level interpretations as interpretive multiplicity or ambiguity. Measuring the degree to which artworks afford such multiplicity presents methodological challenges. Ambiguity is often measured with rating scales, in which viewers judge how ambiguous they perceive a work to be. Such ratings capture how ambiguous a work feels to a given viewer and therefore reflect both the work and the viewer's own perceptual style, knowledge and biases. Qualitative analyses of verbal descriptions offer richer insight into the content of viewers' interpretations, but introduce their own limitations with coding schemes often being developed post hoc, categories depending on researcher judgement, and the resulting data being difficult to systematically compare across studies or sets of stimuli (Muth et al., 2015; Muth & Carbon, 2016). Computational approaches offer a complementary route by aggregating open ended responses across many viewers, thereby providing reproducible metrics that do not depend on a researcher's coding decisions and that can be applied consistently across studies (Iosifyan et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2021).

The present study introduces a sentence-embedding approach to quantify interpretive multiplicity between viewer interpretations. Quantifying the divergence among viewers' descriptions of an artwork indexes the diversity of verbal interpretations it elicits across observers, without relying on viewers' explicit judgments of ambiguity. We complement this artwork-level measure with insight confidence, a viewer's subjective rating of how certain they are that their interpretation captures the full breadth of meanings of an artwork. Insight confidence therefore serves as a viewer-level, metacognitive counterpart to semantic distance.

Together, the two measures allow us to examine artwork-level divergence in interpretations separately from how confident viewers are in their own interpretation.

We examine how this computational measure of interpretive multiplicity relates to aesthetic experience and individual differences in art viewing. Aesthetic experience here refers to a focused engagement with an artwork that can often be emotional and is usually not grounded in its practical utility (Marković, 2012; see Appendix for definitions of key terms used throughout). Below, we discuss the theoretical motivation underlying the current approach.

Semantic Processing in Art Contexts

The perceptual and cognitive system usually strive toward stable interpretations by matching incoming visual signals with predictions derived from pre-existing knowledge about themes, objects and scenes (Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). However, visual stimuli vary considerably in the degree to which they resist such stabilisation, that is, the settling of perception into a single, coherent self-contained meaning with little remaining ambiguity. Visual artworks are often typically presented in an art context that shapes expectations, goals, and modes of processing, leading viewers to engage with them differently than with everyday scenes (Pelowski et al., 2017a).

One consequence is that the art context changes how semantic incongruence is treated. When the same images of everyday objects were presented in an art versus a non-art context, unrelated pairings (e.g., a cup and a brick) were judged as fitting together better in the art context, whereas congruent pairings (e.g., a cup and a spoon) were unaffected (Iosifyan & Wolfe, 2024). Thus, in an art context, an incongruence may not be treated as an error to be rejected. Rather, it can be treated as something to be integrated to form a coherent meaning.

Electrophysiological evidence is consistent with this. Semantic inconsistencies in artworks elicit a later positive response than comparable inconsistencies in real-world photographs (Markey et al., 2019). Late positivities following semantic anomalies are commonly linked to reanalysis and integration of conflicting information (Kutas & Federmeier, 2011). Together, these findings suggest that an art context changes how semantic incongruence is processed, with incongruent or ambiguous information being more readily integrated into a meaningful interpretation than rejected as an error.

Semantic Instability: Conceptualisation and Measurement Challenges

The general property of artworks comprising multiple coexisting interpretations, sometimes even contradictory meanings has been termed Semantic Instability (SeIns; Muth & Carbon, 2016). Muth and Carbon's (2013, 2016) framework proposes that such artworks resist a single, final interpretation and therefore remain open to ongoing elaboration, which may underlie their aesthetic value. Instability can take on different forms. Some initially ambiguous stimuli can be resolved, as when viewers recognise the Dalmatian in a two-tone image (Gregory, 1970; see Figure S1A) or the coherent pattern in a noisy two-tone image in Klimt's Reclining Woman (Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011; see Figure S1B). The resolution of such initially ambiguous stimuli can produce a pleasurable "Aha" experience, where semantic coherence suddenly emerges from perceptual confusion (Muth & Carbon, 2013). Other works remain unresolved, as in Magritte's La Condition Humaine (see Figure S1C), where a painted canvas continues the view behind it so seamlessly that it is unclear which is the depiction and which the scene.

Under predictive processing accounts, artworks high in semantic instability may sustain multiple competing high-level hypotheses about meaning and content, each of which generates partially fulfilled predictions. This may prevent the perceptual system from settling on a single interpretation and maintains ongoing prediction error signals that drive continued engagement (Pepperell, 2024; Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). Given that artworks may be approached with expectations of semantic violations, the following key question emerges: How does the ease with which viewers can arrive at a coherent and stable interpretation of the scene influence their aesthetic experience?

Prior work has characterised the relationship between ambiguity and aesthetic appreciation in partly conflicting ways. Fluency accounts predict that easily interpretable images are liked most (Reber et al., 2004), inverted-U models predict maximal liking at moderate levels of complexity (Berlyne, 1971), and semantic instability theory proposes that challenging, semantically unstable images can enhance aesthetic engagement (Muth et al., 2015; Muth & Carbon, 2016). The evidence is difficult to synthesise because studies define and measure 'ambiguity' or 'complexity' differently, often conflating perceptual complexity, semantic ambiguity, and semantic instability (Muth & Carbon, 2016).

Most studies have also relied on viewers' ratings of perceived ambiguity (Jakesch et al., 2017; Jakesch & Leder, 2009; Mari et al., 2021; Muth et al., 2015). Using this approach, Muth and colleagues (2015) found that perceived ambiguity related positively to interest, perceptual affect, and cognitive affect, in line with the proposal that resisting a single interpretation keeps artworks open to ongoing elaboration (Muth & Carbon, 2013 , 2016; Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011).

However, such ratings conflate the stimulus property (i.e., the interpretive multiplicity an artwork holds) with the viewers' experience of it. This conflation raises conceptual and methodological concerns. Firstly, such ratings rely on the assumption that individuals possess accurate metacognitive insight into their own perceptual and interpretive processes, yet research on meta-cognition has shown that this can be unreliable (Fleming & Lau, 2014; McCurdy et al., 2013; Nisbett & Wilson, 1977). Second, this judgement is grounded in their subjective sense of uncertainty rather than in an objective estimate of the artwork's interpretive multiplicity.

In the present study we therefore quantify this divergence directly, by comparing sentence embeddings of viewers' descriptions of the same artwork (Reimers & Gurevych, 2019). Artworks eliciting more divergent descriptions are taken to show greater interpretive multiplicity at the artwork level, which we treat as one operationalisation of semantic instability. We extend Muth and Carbon's (2016) framework by examining how two distinct operationalisations of semantic instability (semantic distance and insight confidence) relate to aesthetic appreciation (Muth & Carbon, 2016). Throughout, 'interpretive multiplicity' refers to the breadth of viable high-level interpretations an artwork affords, and 'semantic instability' to the broader framework linking such multiplicity to appreciation.

A Computational Approach to Measuring Semantic Instability

Quantifying how far viewers' interpretations diverge requires a way to compare open ended descriptions. One promising tool is sentence-embedding models, which use transformer architectures to map text passages onto vectors such that semantically similar passages lie close together (Pavlyshenko & Stasiuk, 2025; Reimers & Gurevych, 2019; Xiao et al., 2023). Because embeddings capture gradations of semantic relatedness rather than binary categories, the distance between two vectors yields a continuous index of how much two descriptions differ in meaning. Such models are routinely validated against human similarity judgements on the Semantic Textual Similarity (STS) benchmark, in which raters score text pairs from no overlap to equivalence (Cer et al., 2017), and they correlate robustly with those judgements (Reimers & Gurevych, 2019).

Applied to viewer-generated descriptions of the same artwork, the mean pairwise cosine distance between embeddings indexes how widely interpretations diverge across viewers. Artworks eliciting more divergent descriptions show greater interpretive multiplicity and, in the sense used here, greater semantic instability.

The Present Study

The preregistered study had three primary aims (https://osf.io/njdx5). First (RQ1), we developed a computational measure of between-viewer divergence in artwork descriptions, semantic distance, computed as the mean pairwise cosine distance between sentence embeddings of descriptions of the same artwork. Each participant gave one description per artwork, so semantic distance cannot capture the breadth of meanings held by an individual viewer. We therefore also measured insight confidence, the viewer's certainty that their interpretation captures the full meaning of the artwork, as a metacognitive proxy for that breadth. We examined how the two measures relate to each other. Second (RQ2), we examined how these two operationalisations of SeIns relate to aesthetic appreciation on three dimensions: interest, emotional intensity and cognitive affect. We selected these because Muth et al. (2015) found them to have the strongest positive relationships with perceived ambiguity. Third (RQ3), we tested whether ambiguity tolerance moderates these relationships (see Supplement S7 for the full rationale).

We predicted a negative relationship between semantic distance and insight confidence, validating both as meaningful operationalisations of SeIns despite their conceptual distinctness. We further hypothesised that higher semantic distance and lower insight confidence would both be associated with higher aesthetic appreciation scores across all three dimensions: interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect (Muth et al., 2015).

Finally, we predicted that ambiguity tolerance would moderate these relationships, with participants higher in ambiguity tolerance showing stronger positive associations between SeIns measures and aesthetic appreciation.

Method

Participants

Data were collected online via the Prolific platform from 130 participants (M_age = 36.9 years, SD = 12.8, range = 20-77; 83 male, 46 female, 1 undisclosed). Seven participants were excluded due to incomplete data, leaving a final sample of 123 participants included in all analyses. Participants received £9.00 compensation for approximately 50-60 minutes of participation. Eligibility on Prolific was restricted to healthy adults from a set of Western countries, as the stimulus set comprised predominantly Western artworks and cultural familiarity with these artistic traditions was expected to influence interpretive and aesthetic responses (see Supplement S14 for the list of countries).

An a priori simulation-based power analysis for the cross-classified multilevel models, parameterised using variance components and fixed-effect estimates from Muth et al. (2015), indicated that a design with 130 participants each rating 30 artworks yields ≥ 0.89 power to detect the target fixed effects for interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect at α = 0.05, and ≥ 0.80 power for smaller effects under the same model structure.

Materials

Artworks

Sixty artworks were selected from an initial pool of 150 images sourced from online galleries (including the Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam and Museum of Modern Art, New York City) and digital scans from art catalogues. The images contained both abstract and figurative artworks, ranging in date from 1600 to 2017 (see Supplement Table S1 for the entire catalogue of artworks used). A new stimulus set was assembled rather than adopting existing validated sets in order to achieve broad coverage across genres, styles, periods, and artists, to minimise stimulus familiarity effects, and to systematically exclude faces which capture attention preferentially, ensuring suitability for an eye-tracking paradigm. To balance representation across the visual feature space, we applied PCA-based diversity sampling on 46 quantitative image properties (Redies et al., 2025) and drew 12 artworks at random from each of five diversity quintiles (see Supplement S11).

Stimuli were presented on a grey background at a screen resolution of 1024 × 768 pixels. Image size on screen varied as a function of the original artwork dimensions and aspect ratio but was constrained to fit within 80% of the display while preserving the original aspect ratio; only portrait-oriented artworks were chosen and presented in original orientation to avoid orientation related effects.

Aesthetic Appreciation Measures

Three dimensions of aesthetic appreciation were assessed using 7-point Likert scales ranging from 1 (not at all) to 7 (very much), following Muth et al. (2015). Participants rated how interesting they found each artwork (Interest), how strongly the artwork affected them emotionally (Emotional Intensity), and how much the artwork affected their thoughts (Cognitive affect). Cognitive affect was accompanied by a brief explanation clarifying that it referred to whether the artwork made them question what they were looking at and consider multiple possible interpretations. Although conceptually close to interpretive multiplicity, it indexes the viewer's felt cognitive engagement, whereas semantic distance indexes observed divergence between different viewers' descriptions (Muth et al., 2015).

Description and Insight Confidence

Participants were asked to describe any insights they gained from an artwork and if they felt unable to do so, they were instructed to provide a purely physical description instead. Afterward, they rated their interpretive confidence on a 7-point Likert scale (1 = not at all confident,7 = very confident) in response to the question: "How confident are you that your interpretation sufficiently explains this artwork?". This measure is intended to capture participants' subjective certainty that their description fully exhausts the artwork's semantic possibilities rather than representing merely one possible meaning amongst many. Before the experiment, instructions were provided to the participant to score "insight confidence" high if they feel that their description exhausts all possible meanings that the artwork affords, and low if they feel that it can be explained in several other ways.

Individual Difference Measures

Ambiguity Tolerance was measured using the MSTAT-II (McLain, 2009), a 13-item questionnaire assessing tolerance across various ambiguous situations (see Supplement Table S2 for the questionnaire items). Items are rated on 5-point Likert scales (1 = strongly disagree,5 = strongly agree). Total scores were calculated by summing all items following standard scoring procedures, with higher scores indicating greater tolerance of ambiguity.

Art Interest was measured using Part A of the VAIAK (Specker et al., 2020 (Specker et al., , 2023)), comprising 11 items assessing behavioural and motivational dimensions of engagement with visual art. Items are rated on 7-point Likert scales (see Supplement Table S3). Total scores were calculated according to standard VAIAK scoring procedures, with higher scores indicating greater art interest.

Design and Procedure

The study employed a counterbalanced between-items design, in which response task type was assigned across artworks and counterbalanced between participant groups. Each participant viewed all 60 artworks for a fixed duration of 6 seconds per artwork, but the type of response required varied across artworks. Based on feedback from a pilot study, participants were randomly assigned to one of two counterbalancing groups to reduce workload.

In Group 1, artworks 1-30 were followed by interest and emotional intensity ratings, whilst artworks 31-60 were followed by cognitive affect ratings, open-ended descriptions, and insight confidence ratings. Participants were instructed to keep descriptions brief, but no explicit word limit was imposed. Group 2 received the reverse assignment, such that artworks 1-30 received cognitive affect, description, and insight confidence responses, whilst artworks 31-60 received interest and emotional intensity ratings (see Figure 2A). Because of this counterbalancing, every artwork was described and rated for insight confidence and 'cognitive affect' by one group of participants and rated for 'interest' and 'emotional-intensity' by the other group. The split also served to shorten the task (30 artworks per response type per participant). Experimental Design and Trial Sequence 13 Note. (A) Both groups viewed all 60 artworks; response type alternated between the first and second halves, in opposite order across groups. Green = interest, yellow = emotional intensity, blue = cognitive affect, pink = description plus confidence. (B) Trial sequence: 2 s fixation, 6 s artwork, then a self-paced response screen.

The experimental session began with a pre-task questionnaire in which participants completed the VAIAK Part A to assess art interest. They then completed the main task, in which each trial consisted of a 2-second fixation cross, 6 seconds of artwork presentation, and a response screen that remained visible until a response was given (see Figure 2B). Depending on the counterbalanced condition, participants either rated interest and emotional intensity or rated cognitive affect, provided an open-ended description, and rated their insight confidence (see Figure 2A). After all the 60 trials, participants completed a post-task questionnaire comprising the MSTAT-II to assess ambiguity tolerance, which was administered at the end of the study to avoid priming or biasing participants with respect to ambiguity.

Data Analysis

Analyses were run in R (lme4; Bates et al., 2015; lmerTest; (Kuznetsova et al., 2017) and Python (sentence-transformers). Full software versions are listed in Supplement S12. For each artwork, pairwise cosine similarities between participant description embeddings were computed, and semantic distance was defined as 1 -cosine similarity. This ranges between 0 (identical meaning) through 1 (orthogonal or no shared content) and 2 (opposite meaning). Aggregate semantic distance per artwork was computed as the mean of all pairwise distances for that artwork.

We analysed the data using cross-classified linear mixed-effects models, with individual ratings (Level 1) simultaneously nested within participants and artworks (Level 2).

For RQ1, trial-wise insight confidence ratings (1-7) were regressed onto standardised artwork-level semantic distance (mean pairwise cosine distance across all verbal descriptions of each artwork), with random intercepts for both participants and artworks. This tested whether artworks that elicited more interpretively divergent descriptions were associated with systematically lower insight confidence ratings across raters.

For RQ2, we examined how semantic distance and insight confidence (SeIns) relate to aesthetic appreciation (interest, emotional intensity, cognitive affect). Artwork-level mean semantic distance and artwork-level mean insight confidence were computed from the description data, standardised, and used as fixed effects predicting trial-wise appreciation ratings on all three scales, with random intercepts for participants and artworks. Random-slope structures were selected as described in Supplement S3. Both predictors are artwork-level variables.

Ambiguity tolerance (MSTAT-II) and art interest (VAIAK-A) were entered as standardised participant-level predictors and tested as moderators by adding SeIns × MSTAT-II and SeIns × VAIAK-A interaction terms to the mixed-effects models. We used likelihood-ratio tests, AIC differences, and changes in marginal R² to judge whether interactions improved model fit, and we summarised interaction coefficients and p-values in tables.

To control for multiple comparisons across the six confirmatory tests (three outcome measures × two SeIns predictors), we applied a Bonferroni correction, setting the significance threshold at α = .008 (.05/6). Moderation analyses involving MSTAT-II and an exploratory VAIAK-A interaction terms which were each corrected across six tests using a Bonferroni correction (α = .008). Exploratory analyses added quantitative image properties from the Aesthetics Toolbox as artwork-level predictors, with multiple-testing correction within outcome (Supplement S13).

Results

Descriptive statistics for all study variables are presented in Table 1 Table 1 Descriptive Statistics for All Study Variables

Relationship between insight confidence and semantic distance

To account for the hierarchical structure of the data, we fitted a linear mixed-effects model predicting trial-level insight confidence from artwork-level semantic distance (standardised), based on 3,673 observations from 123 participants and 60 artworks. The final model included random intercepts for participants and artworks and random slopes for semantic distance by participant. Higher semantic distance significantly predicted lower insight confidence in this multilevel model, b = -0.29, SE = 0.05, t(72.10) = -6.31, p < .001, β = -.17, marginal R² = .03 (see Figure 3C). Simple trial-level and artwork-level regressions and model diagnostics showed the same pattern (see Figure 3 and Supplement S1).

A quadratic trend was driven by three influential artworks and was not supported after their exclusion (Supplement S2); the linear model was therefore retained. The random-slope model fitted best (see Supplement S3); 38% of the variance in insight confidence was attributable to participants, 6% to artworks, and 56% to residual variance.

Relationship between semantic distance, insight confidence and aesthetic appreciation

Confirmatory Results

Residual diagnostics are reported in Supplement S4. Notably, around half of participants showed negative individual slopes of insight confidence on each outcome (49.6-52.0%), indicating considerable between-person heterogeneity.

For the variable 'interest', a linear mixed-effects model with random slopes for semantic distance by participant showed that higher semantic distance predicted lower interest, b = -0.32, SE = 0.08, t(69.21) = -4.01, p < .001, β = -.19, marginal R² = .04, and conditional R² = .44 (see Figure 4A). A corresponding model with random slopes for insight confidence indicated that higher insight confidence predicted higher interest, b = 0.38, SE = 0.08, t (74.70) = 4.98, p < .001, β = .23, marginal R² = .05, and conditional R² = .45 (see Figure 4B).

For the variable 'emotional intensity', higher semantic distance again predicted lower ratings, b = -0.33, SE = 0.07, t(70.27) = -4.63, p < .001, β = -.19, marginal R² = .04, and conditional R² = .48 (see Figure 4C). Higher insight confidence was associated with higher emotional intensity, b = 0.40, SE = 0.07, t(81.51) = 6.01, p < .001, β = .24, marginal R² = .06, and conditional R² = .49 (see Figure 4D).

For cognitive affect, semantic distance and insight confidence showed smaller but reliable effects. Higher semantic distance predicted lower cognitive affect, b = -0.23, SE = 0.06, t(68.36) = -3.88, p < .001, β = -.14, marginal R² = .02, and conditional R² = .39 (see Figure 4E). Higher insight confidence predicted higher cognitive affect, b = 0.21, SE = 0.06, t(79.69) = 3.34, p = .001, β = .13, marginal R² = .02, and conditional R² = .40 (see Figure 4F).

Following Bonferroni correction (adjusted α = .008), semantic distance and insight confidence significantly predicted the variables 'interest', 'emotional intensity' and 'cognitive affect' (see Supplement Figure S2 and Table S4 for all Bonferroni corrected results).

For all three outcomes, adding either predictor improved fit over a random-intercepts null model, and random slopes by participant improved fit further (see Supplement S3).

Quadratic and cubic terms did not improve any of the three models (all p > .05), so linear models were retained (see Supplement S2). Relationships of Semantic Distance and Insight Confidence With Aesthetic Ratings Note. (A, B) Interest ratings. (C, D) Emotional intensity ratings. (E, F) Cognitive affect ratings. Left panels (A, C, E) show effects of semantic distance (Standardised cosine distance); right panels (B, D, F) show effects of insight confidence (Standardised 1-7 Likert Scale).

Exploratory Results

In joint models including both predictors, insight confidence remained a positive predictor of interest (b = 0.29, SE = 0.09, β = .18) and emotional intensity (b = 0.31, SE = 0.08, β = .18), but not cognitive affect (b = 0.09, SE = 0.07, p = .22). Semantic distance was no longer significant for interest (b = -0.13, p = .16) or emotional intensity (b = -0.14, p = .11), and its effect on cognitive affect (b = -0.16, SE = 0.07, p = .03) was significant but did not meet the corrected threshold (see Supplement S6).

Ambiguity Tolerance (MSTAT-II) as a moderator

Following Bonferroni correction (α = .008), ambiguity tolerance moderated only the effect of semantic distance on interest (b = 0.07, SE = 0.02, t(3508) = 3.57, p < .001, β = .05, marginal R² = .07, conditional R² = .42; see supplement Figure S3). No other interaction with ambiguity tolerance survived correction (see Supplement S8, Table S4, and Figure S3).

Discussion

The study tested two measures of semantic instability. Firstly, semantic distance, a computational measure of divergence of interpretations across viewers. Secondly, insight confidence, a metacognitive rating of how confident an individual viewer is that their interpretation captures the entirety of the artwork's meaning. In line with our preregistered hypothesis, these two measures were negatively correlated, meaning that artworks eliciting more divergent descriptions were rated with lower insight confidence. We related both to aesthetic appreciation, operationalised as interest, emotional intensity and cognitive affect.

Contrary to our preregistered hypothesis, higher semantic distance was associated with lower interest, emotional intensity and cognitive affect, whereas higher insight confidence was associated with higher scores across the three dimensions. In exploratory joint models, insight confidence was the stronger predictor of interest and emotional intensity, and semantic distance did not explain any unique variance in any outcome. This suggests that what drives interest and emotional intensity is not ambiguity per se, but rather the degree to which the viewer is certain about their own interpretation. Ambiguity tolerance as measured by the MSTAT-II showed only small, outcome-specific moderation effects, limited to interest, while art interest as measured by VAIAK-A, which was included as an exploratory measure showed more consistent moderating effects across the three dimensions (see Supplement S8 for results and S9 for interpretations). Quantitative image properties as measured using the Aesthetics Toolbox (Redies et al., 2025), did not reliably moderate these relationships, indicating that the observed patterns are unlikely to be driven by low-level visual properties (see Supplement S13 for results). In the following sections, we discuss these results in greater detail and consider their theoretical relevance and implications.

Linking Semantic Distance and Insight Confidence

Artworks that gave rise to more divergent descriptions across observers tended to elicit lower insight confidence. This suggests that insight confidence may reflect a viewer's metacognitive sensitivity to the degree of interpretive multiplicity a work affords, that is, an implicit awareness that the work may be understood in fundamentally different ways by different observers.

In the first analysis, semantic distance (an artwork-level measure) was related to triallevel insight confidence (a viewer-level measure). This allowed us to examine how far descriptions diverged across viewers separately from how confident individual viewers were in their own interpretations. It thereby separates, at the level of measurement, the artworklevel and viewer-level aspects of semantic instability discussed by Muth and Carbon (2016).

In the appreciation analyses, however, insight confidence was averaged per artwork, so both predictors operated at the artwork level and these analyses do not speak to viewer-level relationships.

How Ambiguity Shapes Aesthetic Appreciation

In contrast to our hypothesis, across artworks, greater semantic distance was associated with lower aesthetic appreciation (interest, emotional intensity, and cognitive affect) whereas higher insight confidence showed positive associations with these dimensions. In joint models including both predictors, insight confidence remained a positive predictor of interest and emotional intensity and a non-significant positive predictor of cognitive affect. The effects of semantic distance were attenuated or absent for all the measures. Taken together, the results indicate that artworks eliciting more divergent descriptions were appreciated less, and that for interest and emotional intensity this association overlapped substantially with the lower confidence with which such artworks were interpreted. One account consistent with this pattern is that interpretive multiplicity relates to appreciation through the confidence viewers can reach in their interpretation.

This pattern is not consistent with the prediction of semantic instability accounts that artworks affording greater interpretive multiplicity should sustain aesthetic engagement (Muth & Carbon, 2016; Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). Instead, our results align more closely with predictive-processing accounts linking aesthetic pleasure to successful prediction-error minimisation (Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011). This raises a question, if pleasure arises from reducing prediction errors, why would viewers seek out artworks that deliberately violate their expectations? Predictive-processing accounts of play faces the same question. Andersen and colleagues (2023) described play as a behaviour in which agents, freed from the demands of other cognitive systems, deliberately seek out or create surprising situations, and propose that play feels good because this surprise is resolved faster than expected. On this view, surprise is rewarding not in itself but as an opportunity for its rapid resolution. Art viewing can be understood as a similar form of cognitive play where viewers enter an art context in which semantic violations are anticipated (Muth et al., 2017), and explore these surprises without practical consequences, consistent with the view that aesthetic experience is usually not grounded in practical utility (Marković, 2012). If so, what may drive aesthetic appreciation may not be uncertainty per se but the process of transforming uncertainty into a coherent, meaningful interpretation (Pelowski et al., 2017b). Artworks that elicit divergent interpretations across viewers may make such resolution harder, whereas artworks that viewers interpret confidently allow it. Under this account, it may not be ambiguity itself but the progress towards a coherent and confident interpretation that drives aesthetic appreciation. As the present design did not assess the underlying processing dynamics, this aspect remains speculative.

This account also connects our results to previous work treating ambiguity as beneficial for aesthetic appreciation. Muth and colleagues (2015) found that the strength of insight experiences during elaboration of ambiguous artworks was positively associated with liking, interest, and both perceptual and cognitive affect, which parallels the positive associations we observed for insight confidence. Two methodological differences may explain why our ambiguity findings nevertheless diverge from theirs. Their ambiguity and insight measures were collected within participants and concerned perceptual structure, whereas ours were derived from verbal descriptions and linked to appreciation ratings from other participants at the artwork level. Our results therefore do not speak to whether the ambiguity a viewer experiences relates to that same viewer's appreciation.

Although insight confidence was positively associated with appreciation at the population level, roughly half of participants showed negative individual slopes. This pattern may be because insight confidence was rated by one group of participants and averaged per artwork, whereas appreciation was rated by another group. These negative slopes do not show that confidence relates negatively to appreciation within any viewer, because no viewer provided both ratings for the same artwork. Associations observed at one level of analysis need not hold at another, particularly when inferences cross levels of explanation (Kievit et al., 2013).

These patterns are compatible with the possibility that positive aesthetic impact often attributed to ambiguity may be driven mainly by the accompanying felt gain in understanding, whether structural or semantic, rather than by ambiguity per se. This interpretation is consistent with recent evidence that active meaning making enhances liking, beauty, and pleasure primarily by reducing perceived uncertainty about an artwork and satisfying viewers' need for certainty (Duan et al., 2026). Importantly, Duan and colleagues (2026) showed that the beneficial effects of certainty did not require eliminating all multiplicity of possible interpretations. Certainty satisfaction was defined as confidence that a painting is meaningful and interpretable, which closely parallels our insight-confidence measure and likewise shows robust links to aesthetic appreciation.

Individual Differences

Whilst the fixed-effect estimate for insight confidence indicated a robust positive association with appreciation at the population level, the random slope distribution revealed substantial between-person variability, with a notable proportion of participants showing negative or near-zero slopes. This suggests that the relationship between metacognitive certainty and aesthetic appreciation is not uniform across viewers. Stable characteristics such as art interest or ambiguity tolerance may determine how long it takes for interpretive resolution and if it is experienced as satisfying.

Ambiguity tolerance moderated only the association between semantic distance and interest. In exploratory analyses, the positive association between artwork-level insight confidence and appreciation was weaker among viewers with greater art interest, for all three outcomes. The negative association between semantic distance and appreciation was likewise weaker among these viewers, but only for interest. In other words, the appreciation of viewers with more art interest depended less on how confidently an artwork was interpreted.

Because the latter analyses were not preregistered, they should be treated as hypothesisgenerating; we discuss both sets of findings in Supplement S9.

Image Features

Quantitative image properties did not significantly moderate the relationships between semantic distance or insight confidence and any of the three appreciation measures (see Supplement S13). These findings suggest that our semantic and metacognitive measures primarily capture higher-level meaning construction that cannot be straightforwardly predicted from formal image properties alone, echoing accounts that distinguish low-level visual features from higher-order semantic and contextual factors in aesthetic experience (Brachmann & Redies, 2017; Hayn-Leichsenring et al., 2017; Redies, 2015; Redies et al., 2025).

The toolbox we used for deriving image properties covers global image statistics and does not include standardised measures of perceptual organisation, such as grouping difficulty or figure-ground ambiguity. Developing and validating such measures for artworks, and including them in models like the present ones, would allow the role of perceptual ambiguity to be examined alongside that of semantic ambiguity in future work.

Limitations

The study has several limitations. Data were collected online, which limited control over viewing conditions, attention and art context, all of which are known to shape aesthetic experience (Brieber et al., 2014; Muth et al., 2017). The sample was recruited via Prolific and restricted to participants from Western cultural backgrounds, and the stimulus set comprised 60 predominantly Western artworks, which limits generalisation to other populations and artistic traditions. At the same time, the stimulus set spans abstract and figurative artworks ranging from 1600 to 2017, includes both colour and black and white artworks, and contains mostly lesser-known artists to reduce the familiarity effect. It was also sampled to cover the visual feature space defined by quantitative image properties from the Aesthetics Toolbox (Redies et al., 2025). The full catalogue is presented (see Supplement Table S1), and the artworks can be provided to future researchers upon request.

The counterbalanced between-items design kept the task short and participants either gave descriptions, rated 'insight confidence', and 'cognitive affect' or rated 'interest' and 'emotional-intensity' but not both. This means that all predictors and outcome variables were not measured within the same person for the same artwork, rather between participants. The conclusions we draw therefore report ambiguity as a property of the artwork, rather than the individual. This does limit our conclusions on variability and causal inference about how semantic instability shapes aesthetic appreciation at the level of the individual. The six second presentation time is both likely short for more complex works, and somewhat unnatural as a viewing condition, so our measures probably capture relatively early stages of interpretation rather than extended engagement, which limits ecological validity compared with self-paced museum viewing. However, the fixed 6 s duration is consistent with established practice in empirical aesthetics (Bradley et al., 2011; Husselman et al., 2024) ensuring the paradigm can be directly extended to future studies incorporating physiological measures.

The study addresses semantic ambiguity and does not address perceptual ambiguity.

Semantic distance indexes divergence in verbal interpretations and therefore reflects ambiguity only to the extent that it is expressed in words. Descriptions may include perceptual content such as shapes, colours or composition, but the measure cannot distinguish whether divergence arises from perceptual ambiguity (e.g., difficulties in grouping, segregation or figure-ground organisation), from interpretive multiplicity, or from viewers attending to different features. Abstract works, which contain no intended recognisable objects, are within the scope of the measure insofar as viewers describe the associations, moods or meanings they perceive. However, semantic distance cannot separate such interpretive divergence from differences in which features viewers chose to mention, in whether they gave interpretive or purely physical descriptions, or in description length and style.

Whilst insight confidence was incorporated as a metacognitive proxy for withinperson semantic instability, metacognitive self-reports reflect viewers' subjective sense of interpretive resolution rather than direct evidence of it, and individual differences in metacognitive accuracy mean that high confidence may in some cases reflect premature closure rather than genuine resolution of competing meanings.

Directions for Future Research

Beyond the present findings, the description-based approach has methodological implications. Because semantic distance is derived from open-ended descriptions, it does not depend on task-specific rating scales or predefined response options. It translates the divergence between the dominant interpretations that viewers arrive at into a continuous, model-based index of interpretive multiplicity. In principle, the same procedure can be applied wherever people provide free-text interpretations of artworks, social scenes or narratives. Computing the semantic distance between these responses yields a stimulus-level estimate of how ambiguous each item is across observers. These estimates can be used to select or match stimuli on ambiguity, compare ambiguity across stimulus sets or audiences, or to test whether ambiguity predicts other responses, as we did here for aesthetic appreciation.

A comparable approach is already used in creativity research, where embedding-based semantic distance serves as a scalable, automated index of originality and divergent thinking (Beaty & Johnson, 2021; Organisciak et al., 2023). Because semantic distance only requires free-text descriptions, it can also be collected outside the laboratory, for instance in museums, galleries or online platforms. Museums could use such descriptions to identify works that elicit highly divergent readings and then test whether labels, guided tours or interactive formats help visitors to resolve that divergence and thereby increase appreciation (Duan et al., 2026).

Future work could combine semantic distance and insight confidence with eye tracking, building on evidence that interpretive difficulty predicts more extensive exploration of contemporary paintings (Ganczarek et al., 2022; Szubielska et al., 2021). EEG studies could further assess whether semantically unstable artworks elicit N400-like responses to ongoing meaning search (Kutas & Federmeier, 2011), and prolonged P600-like semantic reanalysis (Markey et al., 2019). Experiments that manipulate predictive uncertainty, for example by revealing parts of an ambiguous artwork or stimulus are needed to test predictive processing accounts directly (Pelowski et al., 2017b; Van De Cruys & Wagemans, 2011).

Conclusion

The present study introduced a computational, description-based measure of semantic distance alongside a metacognitive measure of insight confidence to characterise semantic instability across viewers and in viewers' confidence in their interpretations. Because semantic distance can be collected unobtrusively from free-text descriptions alone, it offers a scalable measure of interpretive multiplicity and artwork ambiguity. Contrary to our preregistered hypothesis, artworks that elicited more divergent descriptions, and artworks that viewers interpreted with less insight confidence were appreciated less. Therefore, ambiguity in art may be valued less for itself than for the meaning viewers manage to make of it.

Glossary of Key Terms

Term Definition Aesthetic experience A focused engagement with an artwork that can often be emotional and is usually not grounded in its practical utility (Marković, 2012).

Aesthetic appreciation

The study's umbrella term for viewers' evaluative responses to an artwork, operationalised as ratings of interest, emotional intensity and cognitive affect.

Interest How interesting a viewer found an artwork (7-point scale; Muth et al., 2015).

Emotional intensity

How strongly an artwork affected the viewer emotionally (7-point scale; Muth et al., 2015). Behavioural and motivational engagement with visual art, measured with the 11-item Part A of the VAIAK (Specker et al., 2020 (Specker et al., , 2023)).

Quantitative image properties (QIPs)

Computationally derived global image statistics, extracted with the Aesthetics Toolbox (Redies et al., 2025). They were used for stimulus sampling and exploratory analyses.

Beyond these hypotheses, we conducted exploratory analyses to examine whether art interest (VAIAK-A; see Supplement S7) moderates responses to SeIns and whether quantitative image properties (QIPs) extracted using the Aesthetics Toolbox (Redies et al., 2025) relate to semantic distance and aesthetic appreciation. These exploratory analyses first address whether general art engagement and exposure can predict art appreciation. Second, they investigate whether low-level visual features predict high-level semantic processing and whether these relationships operate independently of subjective aesthetic responses.

Figure2

Semantic distance was computed from participants' open-ended artwork descriptions using sentence embeddings, which map variable-length text to fixed-dimensional vectors while preserving semantic relationships. We employed the BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5 BERT-based sentence embedding transformer model (Xiao et al., 2023), which produces 1024dimensional embeddings and shows strong performance on large-scale semantic textual similarity benchmarks. Descriptions were cleaned (whitespace trimmed, spelling errors manually corrected) and encoded with the BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5 model using its default settings (see Supplement S10).

Distributional properties of the MSTAT-II and VAIAK-A scores are reported in Supplement S7. Participants did show lower interest in the second half of the experiment, but the other appreciation measures didn't vary over time (see Supplement S5)

Figure 4

Figure4

Cognitive affect How much an artwork affected the viewer's thoughts, i.e. made them question what they were looking at and consider multiple interpretations (7-point scale;Muth et al., 2015). It indexes felt cognitive engagement, not divergence between viewers. artworks comprising multiple coexisting, sometimes contradictory, meanings that resist a single, final interpretation(Muth & Carbon, 2016). Here it denotes the broader framework linking interpretive multiplicity to appreciation, operationalised through semantic distance and insight confidence. Semantic distance Artwork-level measure of interpretive multiplicity: the mean pairwise cosine distance (1 -cosine similarity) between sentence embeddings of different viewers' descriptions of the same artwork. Higher values indicate more divergent descriptions. Sentence embedding A fixed-length vector representation of a text, produced by a transformer model, such that semantically similar texts lie close together. This study used BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5, which produces 1024-dimensional embeddings (Xiao et al., 2023). Insight confidence Viewer-level metacognitive rating (1-7) of how confident a viewer is that their interpretation sufficiently explains an artwork, i.e. exhausts its possible meanings. It was averaged per artwork when used to dimension capturing the range of reactions to ambiguous stimuli or situations, from rejection to attraction. It was measured with the 13-item MSTAT-II(McLain, 2009).
